What was I trying to say here?” It’s frustrating because I don’t always notice the moment I get pulled off track—I notice after I’ve already lost the time.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find yourself unable to recall what happened or what you did over a longer period of time, like hours or days?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I mean, I don’t think I lose whole hours or days where I can’t account for what happened. If you asked me what I did yesterday, I might be a little fuzzy on the exact order of things or I might have to reconstruct it from context, like, “Okay, I was at my desk, then I must have worked on that,” but it’s not like I come to and have no idea where the time went for an entire afternoon or anything like that.\n\nIt’s more short-term, I guess—like losing the thread of what I was doing in the moment. Over longer periods, I usually know the general shape of the day. Maybe not every detail, but I don’t think I’ve had that experience of not remembering hours or days.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious when you're working on tasks that require attention to detail?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure how to separate anxiety from just… frustration or pressure, if that makes sense. I don’t think I’m sitting there feeling panicky every time I have to do something detailed. It’s more that if I know it’s something where a small mistake would be obvious or embarrassing, I can feel kind of tense about it.\n\nLike I’ll be trying to check carefully, and part of me is thinking, “Don’t miss anything, don’t miss anything,” but at the same time that doesn’t always make me check better. Sometimes it almost makes the task feel heavier, because I’m aware that I’ve missed small things before. But I wouldn’t say anxiety is always the main thing. A lot of the time I only really feel bad after, when I find out I overlooked something. Then I get upset with myself and replay it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your mind wandering or getting distracted is the main reason you're making these small mistakes?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think so. I mean, not every single mistake, probably, but a lot of them seem to happen when my attention has kind of slipped without me noticing.\n\nLike if I’m reading or writing at my desk, I can start out actually trying to focus, and then something small pulls me away—a siren outside, or someone talking in the hallway, or even just some random thought like an exhibit idea or how something could be arranged visually. And I don’t immediately realize I’ve stopped paying attention to what’s in front of me. It’s more like I come back later and notice, “Oh, I’ve been on the same paragraph for twenty minutes,” or I wrote something but didn’t really track what I was doing.\n\nSo with small mistakes, it feels like that’s often the reason. My mind goes somewhere else, and then I’m still technically sitting there doing the task, but I’m not fully with it. Then I miss a detail that I probably would’ve caught if I had stayed locked in the whole time.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your mistakes and difficulty staying focused are interfering with your daily life or causing you a lot of distress?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think it is interfering, even if I sometimes try to tell myself it’s not that big of a deal. It’s not like my whole life has fallen apart or anything, but there have been real consequences.\n\nI’ve turned in work late because I either underestimated how long it would take, or I got stuck trying to focus and then lost time, and by the time I realized where I was, I was already behind. I’ve also missed a couple of application deadlines, which felt really bad because they weren’t things I didn’t care about. I did care. I just didn’t keep on top of them properly, and then suddenly the deadline had passed.\n\nAnd one of my mentors actually told me that my follow-through is starting to hurt opportunities I should be ready for. That kind of hit me pretty hard, because it made it feel less like just a private frustration and more like other people are noticing that I’m not keeping up in the way they expect me to. So, yeah, I’d say it causes distress. Maybe not every minute of every day, but enough that I worry I’m wasting chances I shouldn’t be wasting.\n\nDoctor: When you find out you've made one of these small mistakes, how do you usually react to yourself about it?\n\nPatient: Usually I get really frustrated with myself, honestly. It’s not just, “Oh, I made a typo,” and then I move on. It feels more like this sinking feeling, because a lot of the mistakes are small enough that they seem avoidable, but they’re embarrassing when someone else sees them.\n\nLike, what finally made me come in was that I kept turning in applications or research notes with these little errors that looked careless—a mislabeled artwork date, or I’d skip a question on a form, things like that. And the worst part is that I usually did think I had checked them. So when I find out afterward, I’m like, “How did I miss that? I literally looked over it.” Then I start replaying the whole thing in my head and sort of beating myself up, because it makes me feel irresponsible or sloppy, even though I wasn’t trying to rush through it or ignore it.\n\nI’ll sometimes feel embarrassed before anyone even says anything, because I imagine how it looks from the outside. Like they don’t know that I thought I checked it; they just see the mistake. So my reaction is mostly self-critical—annoyed, embarrassed, and kind of discouraged. It makes me trust myself less the next time I have to submit something.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any impact on your relationships with friends, family, or colleagues due to your mistakes or difficulty staying focused?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think it has, though maybe more in the colleague or mentor side than, like, with friends. I don’t know if “relationships” is exactly the word I would have used at first, because it feels more like people’s confidence in me gets affected, if that makes sense.\n\nWith mentors or people I’m trying to work with, the late work and missed deadlines have definitely created tension. I’ve submitted things late, and I’ve missed a couple of application deadlines, and even when no one is yelling at me or anything, I can feel that it changes how reliable I seem. One of my mentors actually told me that my follow-through is starting to hurt opportunities I should be ready for, and that was… pretty hard to hear. Not because it was unfair, exactly, but because I knew there was truth to it.\n\nSo I guess the impact is that I worry people see me as careless or not serious enough, when I really am trying. And then I get more nervous around them afterward, because I feel like I’ve already made myself look unreliable. It’s not like I’ve had some huge falling-out with anyone, but it does affect how I interact with people, especially professionally or academically.\n\nDoctor: Have you been experiencing these problems with focus and making small mistakes for a short period, or has it been going on for a while?\n\nPatient: It’s been going on for a while, not just like a bad week or one stressful month. I started really noticing it after I moved to Boston, which was after I finished my bachelor’s. So… I guess when I count it out, it’s been about a year and eleven months now.\n\nAt first I kind of thought it was just the transition, like being in a new place and trying to get my footing, so I didn’t label it as a pattern right away. But looking back, it’s been pretty consistent since then. It’s not something that suddenly appeared last month. It’s been this ongoing thing for almost two years.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself repeatedly checking your work or tasks even after you've already completed them, due to a fear of making mistakes?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure how to answer that, because I do check things, but I don’t know if I’d describe it as repeatedly checking out of fear in the way you might mean.\n\nLike, if I’m submitting something important, I’ll usually look it over because I know I miss little things. But it’s not usually this ritual where I’m going back ten times or I can’t stop myself from checking. It’s more like I’ll read through it, think I’ve caught everything, and then somehow there’s still a mistake later. Sometimes after that happens a few times, I’ll feel more anxious the next time and maybe check it again, but I wouldn’t say I’m constantly rechecking completed tasks over and over.\n\nSo… I guess yes, I try to check, but I haven’t really noticed a pattern of compulsively rechecking. It’s more that even when I do check, it doesn’t always work, which is part of what frustrates me.\n\nDoctor: Do you have recurring thoughts or images that are disturbing or intrusive, and that you try to suppress or ignore?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, no. I mean, I do get thoughts that are stressful, like worrying I forgot something or that I made a mistake, but I don’t think they’re disturbing in the sense of images or thoughts that feel really intrusive or scary, if that’s what you mean.\n\nI’m trying to think of an example, and nothing really comes to mind where I’m having some repeated unwanted image or thought that I’m actively trying to suppress. It’s more just normal worry or frustration after I realize I missed something. So I’d say I haven’t really noticed that happening.\n\nDoctor: Are you finding it harder to enjoy or participate in activities you normally like due to your concerns about making mistakes or your difficulty staying focused?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think it does get in the way, maybe less like “I don’t enjoy anything anymore” and more like I end up not fully participating, or I avoid things because I’m behind or worried I’m going to mess up the practical parts.\n\nFor example, because of this stuff I’ve submitted work late, even when I cared about it and wanted to do well. I’ve also missed a couple of application deadlines, which feels really bad because those are things I actually wanted to pursue. It’s not that I didn’t care about the opportunities. I did. I just didn’t keep track of all the steps well enough, or I kept thinking I had more time, and then suddenly it was too late.\n\nAnd I had a mentor tell me recently that my follow-through is starting to hurt opportunities I should be ready for. That was kind of hard to hear, because it made it feel less like just me being frustrated with myself and more like other people are noticing that I’m not keeping up. So, yes, I’d say it’s interfering, even with things I normally want to be involved in.\n\nDoctor: Are your difficulties with focus and making mistakes significantly impacting your ability to function in your daily life or causing you a lot of emotional distress?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think it is impacting me, even if I’d maybe call it mild compared to what some people deal with. It’s not like my whole life has fallen apart, but it’s definitely not just a small annoyance either.\n\nThe main way I notice it is with things that have deadlines or require multiple steps. I’ve turned in work late because I either underestimated how long it would take, or I lost track of the final details, or I thought I was basically done and then realized too late that I wasn’t. And I’ve missed a couple of application deadlines, which really bothered me because those were opportunities I actually wanted. It wasn’t that I didn’t care — I did care — I just didn’t manage the follow-through well enough.\n\nAnd recently a mentor told me that my follow-through is starting to hurt opportunities I should be ready for, and that kind of stuck with me. It was embarrassing, honestly, because I already felt frustrated with myself, but hearing someone else say it made it feel more real. So emotionally, yeah, it causes stress and disappointment, mostly because I feel like I’m capable of doing these things, but then I keep dropping pieces of them.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself excessively preoccupied with details, to the point where it interferes with the overall task or your ability to complete it?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, not in that exact way. I mean, I can get caught up in details sometimes, but it’s usually not because I’m trying to make everything perfect or because one tiny thing has to be done a certain way. It’s more like I lose track of what the main next step is, or I spend too long figuring out where to start.\n\nIf you mean like, do I obsess over formatting or wording or small details until the whole task can’t move forward… I don’t think that’s really the pattern. I might reread something or tweak it, but I wouldn’t say I’m excessively preoccupied with details in a way that feels like the main problem. Usually the issue is more that I’m trying to manage all the pieces and I don’t keep the overall structure in my head very well.\n\nSo… no, I haven’t really noticed that as a major thing. If anything, I’m probably more likely to miss details than get stuck on them.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that your mistakes and difficulties with focus are primarily due to disorganization and lack of persistence, or are they more related to a fear of making mistakes or a need for perfection?\n\nPatient: I think it’s much more disorganization than fear of mistakes or needing it to be perfect. Like, I do feel bad when I make mistakes, obviously, and I can get embarrassed about it, but the thing that’s actually happening most of the time is more chaotic than perfectionistic.\n\nMy apartment and my laptop kind of show it, honestly. They both look like a bunch of half-started projects. I’ll have rent papers in one pile, gallery notes somewhere else, article PDFs open or saved in random places, and then multiple to-do lists that don’t totally match each other. And then I’ll sit down to do something and it’s like… I don’t know what the real first step is anymore. I can see all the pieces, but I can’t sort them into an order quickly enough, so I bounce between them or delay starting.\n\nSo I wouldn’t say I’m stuck because I’m afraid it won’t be perfect. It’s more like I lose the structure of the task, or I don’t keep track of what needs to happen next, and then things slip.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah, I do procrastinate, but I think it’s less because I need things to be perfect and more because I can feel how much mental effort something is going to take, and then I sort of avoid it.

Like if it’s something straightforward, I can usually make myself do it eventually. But if I know it’s going to require sustained concentration — like drafting a curatorial proposal, where I have to organize my ideas and make it sound coherent, or reading a really dense theory chapter where I know I’ll have to slow down and actually process it — I’ll suddenly find all these smaller things that feel easier to do. I’ll answer emails, tidy up a little, check some random errand, reorganize notes, make coffee, make a better to-do list… and it feels productive in the moment, so I can kind of justify it to myself.

But really I’m avoiding the bigger task because it feels mentally heavy. And then it gets pushed later and later until it’s suddenly the night before it’s due, and then I’m stressed and trying to force myself through it all at once. So yes, definitely procrastinating because it feels overwhelming, but not really in a “this has to be perfect” way. More like, “I don’t know if I have the focus for this right now,” so I keep circling around it instead of starting.

Like if I’m at my desk trying to read something or write—especially if it’s something that needs careful attention—I’ll be in the middle of it, and then a siren goes by outside, or I hear someone talking in the hallway, and my attention just sort of… follows it.\n\nAnd it’s not always external stuff either. I mean, not every single mistake, probably, but a lot of them seem to happen when my attention has kind of slipped without me noticing.\n\nLike if I’m reading or writing at my desk, I can start out actually trying to focus, and then something small pulls me away—a siren outside, or someone talking in the hallway, or even just some random thought like an exhibit idea or how something could be arranged visually. I literally looked over it.” Then I start replaying the whole thing in my head and sort of beating myself up, because it makes me feel irresponsible or sloppy, even though I wasn’t trying to rush through it or ignore it.\n\nI’ll sometimes feel embarrassed before anyone even says anything, because I imagine how it looks from the outside. Like they don’t know that I thought I checked it; they just see the mistake. So my reaction is mostly self-critical—annoyed, embarrassed, and kind of discouraged. One of my mentors actually told me that my follow-through is starting to hurt opportunities I should be ready for, and that was… pretty hard to hear. Sometimes after that happens a few times, I’ll feel more anxious the next time and maybe check it again, but I wouldn’t say I’m constantly rechecking completed tasks over and over.\n\nSo… I guess yes, I try to check, but I haven’t really noticed a pattern of compulsively rechecking. It’s more that even when I do check, it doesn’t always work, which is part of what frustrates me.\n\nDoctor: Do you have recurring thoughts or images that are disturbing or intrusive, and that you try to suppress or ignore?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, no. I mean, I do get thoughts that are stressful, like worrying I forgot something or that I made a mistake, but I don’t think they’re disturbing in the sense of images or thoughts that feel really intrusive or scary, if that’s what you mean.\n\nI’m trying to think of an example, and nothing really comes to mind where I’m having some repeated unwanted image or thought that I’m actively trying to suppress. It’s more just normal worry or frustration after I realize I missed something. So I’d say I haven’t really noticed that happening.\n\nDoctor: Are you finding it harder to enjoy or participate in activities you normally like due to your concerns about making mistakes or your difficulty staying focused?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think it does get in the way, maybe less like “I don’t enjoy anything anymore” and more like I end up not fully participating, or I avoid things because I’m behind or worried I’m going to mess up the practical parts.\n\nFor example, because of this stuff I’ve submitted work late, even when I cared about it and wanted to do well. I’ve also missed a couple of application deadlines, which feels really bad because those are things I actually wanted to pursue. It’s not that I didn’t care about the opportunities. I did. I just didn’t keep track of all the steps well enough, or I kept thinking I had more time, and then suddenly it was too late.\n\nAnd I had a mentor tell me recently that my follow-through is starting to hurt opportunities I should be ready for. That was kind of hard to hear, because it made it feel less like just me being frustrated with myself and more like other people are noticing that I’m not keeping up. So, yes, I’d say it’s interfering, even with things I normally want to be involved in.\n\nDoctor: Are your difficulties with focus and making mistakes significantly impacting your ability to function in your daily life or causing you a lot of emotional distress?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think it is impacting me, even if I’d maybe call it mild compared to what some people deal with. It’s not like my whole life has fallen apart, but it’s definitely not just a small annoyance either.\n\nThe main way I notice it is with things that have deadlines or require multiple steps. I’ve turned in work late because I either underestimated how long it would take, or I lost track of the final details, or I thought I was basically done and then realized too late that I wasn’t. And I’ve missed a couple of application deadlines, which really bothered me because those were opportunities I actually wanted. It wasn’t that I didn’t care — I did care — I just didn’t manage the follow-through well enough.\n\nAnd recently a mentor told me that my follow-through is starting to hurt opportunities I should be ready for, and that kind of stuck with me. It was embarrassing, honestly, because I already felt frustrated with myself, but hearing someone else say it made it feel more real. So emotionally, yeah, it causes stress and disappointment, mostly because I feel like I’m capable of doing these things, but then I keep dropping pieces of them.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself excessively preoccupied with details, to the point where it interferes with the overall task or your ability to complete it?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, not in that exact way. I mean, I can get caught up in details sometimes, but it’s usually not because I’m trying to make everything perfect or because one tiny thing has to be done a certain way. It’s more like I lose track of what the main next step is, or I spend too long figuring out where to start.\n\nIf you mean like, do I obsess over formatting or wording or small details until the whole task can’t move forward… I don’t think that’s really the pattern. I might reread something or tweak it, but I wouldn’t say I’m excessively preoccupied with details in a way that feels like the main problem. Usually the issue is more that I’m trying to manage all the pieces and I don’t keep the overall structure in my head very well.\n\nSo… no, I haven’t really noticed that as a major thing. If anything, I’m probably more likely to miss details than get stuck on them.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that your mistakes and difficulties with focus are primarily due to disorganization and lack of persistence, or are they more related to a fear of making mistakes or a need for perfection?\n\nPatient: I think it’s much more disorganization than fear of mistakes or needing it to be perfect. Like, I do feel bad when I make mistakes, obviously, and I can get embarrassed about it, but the thing that’s actually happening most of the time is more chaotic than perfectionistic.\n\nMy apartment and my laptop kind of show it, honestly. They both look like a bunch of half-started projects. I’ll have rent papers in one pile, gallery notes somewhere else, article PDFs open or saved in random places, and then multiple to-do lists that don’t totally match each other. And then I’ll sit down to do something and it’s like… I don’t know what the real first step is anymore. I can see all the pieces, but I can’t sort them into an order quickly enough, so I bounce between them or delay starting.\n\nSo I wouldn’t say I’m stuck because I’m afraid it won’t be perfect. It’s more like I lose the structure of the task, or I don’t keep track of what needs to happen next, and then things slip.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself procrastinating or avoiding tasks because you feel overwhelmed by the details or the need to do them perfectly?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do procrastinate, but I think it’s less because I need things to be perfect and more because I can feel how much mental effort something is going to take, and then I sort of avoid it.\n\nLike if it’s something straightforward, I can usually make myself do it eventually. But if I know it’s going to require sustained concentration — like drafting a curatorial proposal, where I have to organize my ideas and make it sound coherent, or reading a really dense theory chapter where I know I’ll have to slow down and actually process it — I’ll suddenly find all these smaller things that feel easier to do. I’ll answer emails, tidy up a little, check some random errand, reorganize notes, make coffee, make a better to-do list… and it feels productive in the moment, so I can kind of justify it to myself.\n\nBut really I’m avoiding the bigger task because it feels mentally heavy. And then it gets pushed later and later until it’s suddenly the night before it’s due, and then I’m stressed and trying to force myself through it all at once. So yes, definitely procrastinating because it feels overwhelming, but not really in a “this has to be perfect” way. More like, “I don’t know if I have the focus for this right now,” so I keep circling around it instead of starting.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },

    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

Usually the issue is more that I’m trying to manage all the pieces and I don’t keep the overall structure in my head very well.\n\nSo… no, I haven’t really noticed that as a major thing. If anything, I’m probably more likely to miss details than get stuck on them.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that your mistakes and difficulties with focus are primarily due to disorganization and lack of persistence, or are they more related to a fear of making mistakes or a need for perfection?\n\nPatient: I think it’s much more disorganization than fear of mistakes or needing it to be perfect. Like, I do feel bad when I make mistakes, obviously, and I can get embarrassed about it, but the thing that’s actually happening most of the time is more chaotic than perfectionistic.\n\nMy apartment and my laptop kind of show it, honestly. They both look like a bunch of half-started projects. I’ll have rent papers in one pile, gallery notes somewhere else, article PDFs open or saved in random places, and then multiple to-do lists that don’t totally match each other. And then I’ll sit down to do something and it’s like… I don’t know what the real first step is anymore. I can see all the pieces, but I can’t sort them into an order quickly enough, so I bounce between them or delay starting.\n\nSo I wouldn’t say I’m stuck because I’m afraid it won’t be perfect. It’s more like I lose the structure of the task, or I don’t keep track of what needs to happen next, and then things slip.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself procrastinating or avoiding tasks because you feel overwhelmed by the details or the need to do them perfectly?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do procrastinate, but I think it’s less because I need things to be perfect and more because I can feel how much mental effort something is going to take, and then I sort of avoid it.\n\nLike if it’s something straightforward, I can usually make myself do it eventually. But if I know it’s going to require sustained concentration — like drafting a curatorial proposal, where I have to organize my ideas and make it sound coherent, or reading a really dense theory chapter where I know I’ll have to slow down and actually process it — I’ll suddenly find all these smaller things that feel easier to do. I’ll answer emails, tidy up a little, check some random errand, reorganize notes, make coffee, make a better to-do list… and it feels productive in the moment, so I can kind of justify it to myself.\n\nBut really I’m avoiding the bigger task because it feels mentally heavy. And then it gets pushed later and later until it’s suddenly the night before it’s due, and then I’m stressed and trying to force myself through it all at once. So yes, definitely procrastinating because it feels overwhelming, but not really in a “this has to be perfect” way. More like, “I don’t know if I have the focus for this right now,” so I keep circling around it instead of starting.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you think your mistakes are more due to not paying attention to details or to worrying too much about getting everything just right?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I think it’s much more not paying attention to the details, honestly. Not in a careless-on-purpose way, but I’ll genuinely think I checked something, and then later I’ll notice some small thing that should have been obvious.

That’s actually a lot of why I finally came in. I kept turning in applications or research notes with these little embarrassing mistakes — like I mislabeled the date for an artwork, or I skipped a question on a form even though I was sure I went through the whole thing. And then when I see it afterward, I’m like, how did I miss that? It’s not that I was sitting there agonizing over whether every word was perfect. I usually felt like I had reviewed it enough.

So it feels less like worrying too much about getting everything exactly right, and more like my attention just drops out at the wrong moment. I’ll scan something, but I don’t really catch what’s missing. And because the mistakes are small, they’re easy to overlook until someone else notices, or until it’s already submitted, which is… really frustrating and kind of embarrassing.

And then it gets pushed later and later until it’s suddenly the night before it’s due, and then I’m stressed and trying to force myself through it all at once. More like, “I don’t know if I have the focus for this right now,” so I keep circling around it instead of starting.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your mistakes are more due to not paying attention to details or to worrying too much about getting everything just right?\n\nPatient: I think it’s much more not paying attention to the details, honestly. Not in a careless-on-purpose way, but I’ll genuinely think I checked something, and then later I’ll notice some small thing that should have been obvious.\n\nThat’s actually a lot of why I finally came in. I kept turning in applications or research notes with these little embarrassing mistakes — like I mislabeled the date for an artwork, or I skipped a question on a form even though I was sure I went through the whole thing. And then when I see it afterward, I’m like, how did I miss that? It’s not that I was sitting there agonizing over whether every word was perfect. I usually felt like I had reviewed it enough.\n\nSo it feels less like worrying too much about getting everything exactly right, and more like my attention just drops out at the wrong moment. I’ll scan something, but I don’t really catch what’s missing. And because the mistakes are small, they’re easy to overlook until someone else notices, or until it’s already submitted, which is… really frustrating and kind of embarrassing.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. I’m not totally sure, actually. I wouldn’t say restlessness or fidgeting is the main thing I notice when I’m trying to focus.

I mean, I probably do little things sometimes — like shifting in my chair or messing with a pen or changing position — but I haven’t really paid attention to whether that’s a big pattern for me. It doesn’t stand out the way the focus and organization stuff does. But if I know it’s going to require sustained concentration — like drafting a curatorial proposal, where I have to organize my ideas and make it sound coherent, or reading a really dense theory chapter where I know I’ll have to slow down and actually process it — I’ll suddenly find all these smaller things that feel easier to do. I’ll answer emails, tidy up a little, check some random errand, reorganize notes, make coffee, make a better to-do list… and it feels productive in the moment, so I can kind of justify it to myself.\n\nBut really I’m avoiding the bigger task because it feels mentally heavy. And then it gets pushed later and later until it’s suddenly the night before it’s due, and then I’m stressed and trying to force myself through it all at once. So yes, definitely procrastinating because it feels overwhelming, but not really in a “this has to be perfect” way. More like, “I don’t know if I have the focus for this right now,” so I keep circling around it instead of starting.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your mistakes are more due to not paying attention to details or to worrying too much about getting everything just right?\n\nPatient: I think it’s much more not paying attention to the details, honestly. Not in a careless-on-purpose way, but I’ll genuinely think I checked something, and then later I’ll notice some small thing that should have been obvious.\n\nThat’s actually a lot of why I finally came in. I kept turning in applications or research notes with these little embarrassing mistakes — like I mislabeled the date for an artwork, or I skipped a question on a form even though I was sure I went through the whole thing. And then when I see it afterward, I’m like, how did I miss that? It’s not that I was sitting there agonizing over whether every word was perfect. I usually felt like I had reviewed it enough.\n\nSo it feels less like worrying too much about getting everything exactly right, and more like my attention just drops out at the wrong moment. I’ll scan something, but I don’t really catch what’s missing. And because the mistakes are small, they’re easy to overlook until someone else notices, or until it’s already submitted, which is… really frustrating and kind of embarrassing.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself feeling restless or fidgety when you're trying to focus on a task?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure, actually. I wouldn’t say restlessness or fidgeting is the main thing I notice when I’m trying to focus.\n\nI mean, I probably do little things sometimes — like shifting in my chair or messing with a pen or changing position — but I haven’t really paid attention to whether that’s a big pattern for me. It doesn’t stand out the way the focus and organization stuff does. When I’m stuck on a task, it feels more like my mind is the part that’s jumping around, if that makes sense, not necessarily that my body feels like it has to move.\n\nSo… maybe a little, but I don’t know that I’d describe myself as physically restless most of the time. I’d have to think about it more."
